On generalization of Breuil-Schraen’s ℒ \mathscr{L} -invariants to GL n \mathrm{GL}_{n}
Zicheng Qian

Abstract
. Let p p be prime number and K K be a p p -adic field. We systematically compute the higher Ext \mathrm{Ext} -groups between locally analytic generalized Steinberg representations (LAGS for short) of GL n ​ ( K ) \mathrm{GL}_{n}(K) via a new combinatorial treatment of some spectral sequences arising from the so-called Tits complex. Such spectral sequences degenerate at the second page and each Ext \mathrm{Ext} -group admits a canonical filtration whose graded pieces are terms in the second page of the corresponding spectral sequence. For each pair of LAGS, we are particularly interested their Ext \mathrm{Ext} -groups in the bottom two non-vanishing degrees. We write down an explicit basis for each graded piece (under the canonical filtration) of such an Ext \mathrm{Ext} -group, and then describe the cup product maps between such Ext \mathrm{Ext} -groups using these bases. As an application, we generalize Breuil’s ℒ \mathscr{L} -invariants for GL 2 ​ ( ℚ p ) \mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}) and Schraen’s higher ℒ \mathscr{L} -invariants for GL 3 ​ ( ℚ p ) \mathrm{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}) to GL n ​ ( K ) \mathrm{GL}_{n}(K) . Along the way, we also establish a generalization of Bernstein–Zelevinsky geometric lemma to admissible locally analytic representations constructed by Orlik–Strauch, generalizing a result in Schraen’s thesis for GL 3 ​ ( ℚ p ) \mathrm{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}) .



1.
Introduction

Let
p
be a prime number and
E
be a sufficiently large finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. The theory of
\mathscr{L}
-invariant(s) has a long history, and was first introduced by Mazur-Tate-Teitelbaum
[
MTT86
]
to describe the derivative of the
p
-adic
L
-function (for a certain weight
2
modular form
f
) at its exceptional zero. If we write
\rho_{f}
for the
\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
-representation attached to
f
via Langlands correspondence, then we are interested in the case when
\rho_{f,p}
is semi-stable non-crystalline, and this derivative, written
\mathscr{L}(f)
, depends only on
\rho_{f,p}
and can be read off explicitly from the filtered
(\varphi,N)
-module attached to
\rho_{f,p}
via Fontaine’s theory (see
[
Fon94
]
). We usually use the term
Fontaine-Mazur
\mathscr{L}
-invariant
for invariants (of semi-stable
p
-adic Galois representations) which are defined using an admissible Hodge filtration on a
(\varphi,N)
-module. Among many different definitions of the
\mathscr{L}
-invariant
\mathscr{L}(f)
(which turn out to be equivalent, see the book
[
Ast
]
for a comprehensive study), Breuil constructs in
[
Bre04
]
an explicit finite length locally analytic representation
\Pi(\rho_{f,p})
(written
\Sigma(2,\mathcal{L})
in
loc.it.
with
\mathcal{L}=\mathscr{L}(f)
) of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
whose isomorphism class recovers
\mathscr{L}(f)
, and shows in
[
Bre10
]
that
\Pi(\rho_{f,p})
embeds into the
f
-isotypic component of completed cohomology of modular curves. (Such results are usually called
p
-adic local-global compatibility). Breuil’s construction is actually one of the first instances of the
p
-adic Langlands correspondence, and the map
\rho_{f,p}\mapsto\Pi(\rho_{f,p})
can be improved to be a bijection between the family of semi-stable non crystalline
p
-adic continuous representations
\rho:\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{2}(E)
and an explicit family of locally analytic (
E
-)representations
\Pi(\rho)
of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Motivated by the general philosophy of
p
-adic Langlands correspondence, it is natural to seek for generalizations of Breuil’s
\mathscr{L}
-invariants to
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
with
[K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]<\infty
, namely to recover all Fontaine-Mazur
\mathscr{L}
-invariants of a semi-stable
\rho:\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{K}/K)\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n}(E)
from a certain locally analytic representation
\Pi(\rho)
of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
. When
\rho
is crystalline with generic
\varphi
-eigenvalues, it is expected that (for
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)\neq\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
)
\Pi(\rho)
has to involve some locally analytic representations which do not appear in any parabolic induction. Hence, an explicit construction of
\Pi(\rho)
(which recovers all Fontaine-Mazur
\mathscr{L}
-invariants) is not yet available beyond the case of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. However, according to Breuil’s
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}
conjecture in
[
Bre19
]
, we do expect the existence of some explicit
\Pi(\rho)
that recovers
all
Fontaine-Mazur
\mathscr{L}
-invariants of
\rho
, when
\rho
is semi-stable with maximal rank monodromy (namely
N^{n-1}\neq 0
).

We assume from now that
\rho
is semi-stable with
N^{n-1}\neq 0
. We also assume for simplicity in this introduction that
\rho
is
ordinary
, in which case
\rho
is up to a twist of the following form

(1.1)

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\varepsilon^{n-1}&\cdots&\ast\\
&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)

with
\varepsilon:\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})\rightarrow E^{\times}
being the
p
-adic cyclotomic character.
In
[
Ding19
]
, Ding has constructed locally analytic representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
that recover those Fontaine-Mazur
\mathscr{L}
-invariants of
\rho
coming from a two dimensional subquotient of
\rho
, which are morally the extension parameters at simple roots. The next objects of interest are therefore generalizations of Breuil’s
\mathscr{L}
-invariants that conjecturally corresponds to Fontaine-Mazur
\mathscr{L}
-invariants of
\rho

at non-simple roots
, the so-called
higher
\mathscr{L}
-invariants
. Much work has been done towards higher
\mathscr{L}
-invariants and there are essentially two different approaches which are closely related. Following Breuil’s
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}
conjecture in
[
Bre19
]
, Breuil-Ding construct explicit locally analytic representations
\Pi^{\rm{BD}}(\rho)
of
\mathrm{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
(by which we mean either
\Pi(D)^{-}
or
\Pi(D)
in (3.111) and (3.112) of
loc.it.
with
\lambda=0
), establish their (
p
-adic) local-global compatibility (crucially using
p
-adic Langlands correspondence for
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
) in
[
BD20
]
, and then study their image under certain functor towards
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules in
[
BD19
]
. In another direction, Schraen has done extensive computations in his thesis
[
Schr11
]
and find higher
\mathscr{L}
-invariants inside certain (higher)
\mathrm{Ext}
-groups between locally analytic generalized Steinberg representations. Afterwards, part of the author’s thesis has connected Breuil-Ding and Schraen’s approaches in an explicit way, and in particular leading to a new family of locally analytic representations
\Pi^{\rm{Q}}(\rho)
(see (1.1) of
[
Qian21
]
) which contains
\Pi^{\rm{BD}}(\rho)
. Breuil-Ding’s approach has the advantage of constructing explicit true representations (instead of objects in certain derived category), but its generalization to
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(K)
is à priori difficult due to complexity of
\Pi^{\rm{BD}}(\rho)
and
\Pi^{\rm{Q}}(\rho)
. Schraen’s approach is certainly not producing explicit representations, but has the obvious advantage of involving only locally analytic generalized Steinberg representations in its formulation. So it seems fair to first generalize Schraen’s approach, which is the main focus of this paper.

1.1.
Statement of the main results

We only state our main result for
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
for simplicity. Let
B
(resp.
B^{+}
, resp.
T
) be the lower triangular Borel subgroup (resp. upper triangular Borel subgroup, resp. diagonal maximal torus) of
\mathrm{GL}_{n/\mathbb{Z}}
and
\Delta
be the set of positive simple roots with respect to the pair
B^{+}
,
T
. Then for each subset
I\subseteq\Delta
, we can attach a parabolic subgroup
P_{I}\supseteq B
and a standard Levi subgroup
L_{I}\subseteq\mathrm{GL}_{n}
.
Note that
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
if and only if
P_{I^{\prime}}\subseteq P_{I}
. For each
I\subseteq\Delta
, we define

i_{I}^{\rm{an}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\left(\mathrm{Ind}_{P_{I}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}1_{L_{I}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\right)^{\rm{an}}

and then

v_{I}^{\rm{an}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}i_{I}^{\rm{an}}/\sum_{I^{\prime}\supsetneq I}i_{I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}},

which is the so-called
locally analytic generalized Steinberg representation
attached to
I
. Note that
\mathrm{St}_{n}^{\rm{an}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}v_{\emptyset}^{\rm{an}}
is called the
locally analytic Steinberg representation
and
v_{\Delta}^{\rm{an}}
is the trivial representation
1_{n}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Similarly, for each
I\subseteq\Delta
, we have a
smooth generalized Steinberg representation

v_{I}^{\infty}
(with
\mathrm{St}_{n}^{\infty}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}v_{\emptyset}^{\infty}
) which can be identified with the set of smooth vectors of
v_{I}^{\rm{an}}
.
Each
v_{I}^{\rm{an}}
clearly factors as a locally analytic representation of
\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.
We write
\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{\bullet}(-,-)
for locally analytic
\mathrm{Ext}
-groups between admissible locally analytic representations of
\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
(see Section
1.4
for further details). For each pair of subsets
I^{\prime}\subseteq I\subseteq\Delta
, we write

(1.2)

\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{\#I-\#I^{\prime}}(v_{I}^{\rm{an}},v_{I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}),

and note that each triple
I^{\prime\prime}\subseteq I^{\prime}\subseteq I
induces a cup product map

(1.3)

\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{I^{\prime},I^{\prime\prime}}\xrightarrow{\cup}\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime\prime}}.

The following is our main result

Theorem 1.1

(Theorem
4.22
, Corollary
5.1
)
.

The
\mathrm{Ext}
-groups (
1.2
) and cup product maps (
1.3
) satisfy the following properties.

(i)

for each
\alpha\in\Delta
, we have a canonical isomorphism
\mathbf{E}_{\{\alpha\},\emptyset}\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\rm{cont}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},E)
;

(ii)

for each
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
, we have a canonical isomorphism
\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}\cong\mathbf{E}_{I\setminus I^{\prime},\emptyset}
;

(iii)

the map (
1.3
) is injective, and is an isomorphism if
\alpha+\beta
is not a root for any
\alpha\in I\setminus I^{\prime}
and
\beta\in I^{\prime}\setminus I^{\prime\prime}
;

(iv)

the map (
1.3
) together with item
(ii)
fits into the following commutative diagram

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{\cong}
\displaystyle{\otimes}
\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}_{I^{\prime},I^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}_{I^{*},I^{\prime\prime}}}
\displaystyle{\otimes}
\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{*}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime\prime}}}

with
I^{*}=I^{\prime\prime}\cup(I\setminus I^{\prime})
and
\varepsilon=(-1)^{(\#I\setminus I^{\prime})(\#I^{\prime}\setminus I^{\prime\prime})}
;

(v)

The map

\bigoplus_{\emptyset\neq I^{\prime}\subsetneq I}\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{I^{\prime},\emptyset}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}_{I,\emptyset}

has a codimension one image if
\sum_{\alpha\in I}\alpha
is a root, and is surjective otherwise.

Remark 1.2
.

(1)

We have

\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{k}(v_{I}^{\rm{an}},v_{I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}})=0

for each
k<\#I-\#I^{\prime}
, and thus
\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}
is the bottom degree non-vanishing
\mathrm{Ext}
-group for each
I^{\prime}\subseteq I\subseteq\Delta
.

(2)

The space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\rm{cont}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},E)
admits a basis
\{\mathrm{val},\log\}
where
\mathrm{val}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow E
is
p
-adic valuation and
\log
is any branch of the
p
-adic logarithm. In particular,
\mathrm{Hom}_{\rm{cont}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},E)
contains a canonical line
E\mathrm{val}
spanned by
\mathrm{val}
.

(3)

In Corollary
5.1
, we also study
\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{\#I-\#I^{\prime}+1}(v_{I}^{\rm{an}},v_{I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}})
as well as cup product map of the form

\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{I^{\prime},I^{\prime\prime}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\cup}\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime\prime}}^{\prime},

and Theorem
1.1
has a natural variant in this setting.

(4)

When
n=2
, Theorem
1.1
goes back to
[
Bre04
]
. The
n=3
case is proven by Schraen in
[
Schr11
]
as a key ingredient in his definition of (higher)
\mathscr{L}
-invariants for
\mathrm{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

When
n=2
, we have a natural exact sequence

v_{\Delta}^{\rm{an}}\hookrightarrow i_{\emptyset}^{\rm{an}}\twoheadrightarrow v_{\emptyset}^{\rm{an}}

which induces canonical isomorphisms

(1.4)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\rm{cont}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},E)\cong\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}}^{1}(1,1)\cong\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{1}(i_{\emptyset}^{\rm{an}},i_{\emptyset}^{\rm{an}})\cong\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{1}(v_{\Delta}^{\rm{an}},v_{\emptyset}^{\rm{an}}).

Consequently, given a
E
-line
W\subseteq\mathrm{Hom}_{\rm{cont}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},E)
, Breuil attaches a representation
V_{W}
that fits into

(1.5)

v_{\emptyset}^{\rm{an}}\hookrightarrow V_{W}\twoheadrightarrow v_{\Delta}^{\rm{an}}.

Note that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\rm{cont}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},E)
contains a canonical
E
-line
E\mathrm{val}
, and
W\neq E\mathrm{val}
if and only if
V_{W}
is uniserial if and only if the set of smooth vectors
V_{W}^{\infty}\subseteq V_{W}
is
\mathrm{St}_{2}^{\infty}
if and only if the corresponding
\rho:\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{2}(E)
is semi-stable non-crystalline (namely
N\neq 0
).

Now we consider the case
n=3
which is first studied in
[
Schr11
]
. Similar to (
1.4
), we have canonical isomorphisms

(1.6)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\rm{cont}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},E)\cong\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{1}(v_{\Delta}^{\rm{an}},v_{\Delta\setminus\{\alpha\}}^{\rm{an}})\cong\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{1}(v_{\{\alpha\}}^{\rm{an}},v_{\emptyset}^{\rm{an}}),

and we write
\mathrm{val}_{\alpha}
for the element corresponding to
\mathrm{val}
under (
1.6
) in the second and third space, for each
\alpha\in\Delta
. Consequently, each
E
-line
W_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{1}(v_{\{\alpha\}}^{\rm{an}},v_{\emptyset}^{\rm{an}})
determines a representation
V_{W_{\alpha}}
that fits into

v_{\emptyset}^{\rm{an}}\hookrightarrow V_{W_{\alpha}}\twoheadrightarrow v_{\{\alpha\}}^{\rm{an}}.

We assume that

(1.7)

W_{\alpha}\neq E\mathrm{val}_{\alpha}

for each
\alpha\in\Delta
.
Then Schraen computes that

(1.8)

\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{2}(v_{\Delta}^{\rm{an}},V_{W_{\alpha_{1}},W_{\alpha_{2}}})\cong\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\emptyset}/\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\{\alpha_{i}\}}\cup W_{\alpha_{i}}\right)

is two-dimensional, where
V_{W_{\alpha_{1}},W_{\alpha_{2}}}
is the amalgamate sum of
V_{W_{\alpha_{1}}}
and
V_{W_{\alpha_{2}}}
over
v_{\emptyset}^{\rm{an}}
with
\Delta=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}
. (Note that (
1.7
) for each
\alpha\in\Delta
if and only if the set of smooth vectors
V_{W_{\alpha_{1}},W_{\alpha_{2}}}^{\infty}\subseteq V_{W_{\alpha_{1}},W_{\alpha_{2}}}
is
\mathrm{St}_{3}^{\infty}
.) We abuse
\mathrm{val}_{\alpha_{1}}\cup\mathrm{val}_{\alpha_{2}}
for its image in (
1.8
). Hence, he can choose a third
E
-line
W_{\alpha_{3}}
(with
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
being the unique non-simple positive root) inside (
1.8
) satisfying

(1.9)

W_{\alpha_{3}}\neq E\mathrm{val}_{\alpha_{1}}\cup\mathrm{val}_{\alpha_{2}}

and then define a derived object which (morally) fits into the following distinguished triangle (see (1.14) of
[
Schr11
]
)

(1.10)

V_{W_{\alpha_{1}},W_{\alpha_{2}}}\rightarrow V_{W_{\alpha_{1}},W_{\alpha_{2}},W_{\alpha_{3}}}\rightarrow v_{\Delta}^{\rm{an}}[-1]\rightarrow.

Then he uses (
1.10
) to pin down a filtered
(\varphi,N)
-module from the de Rham complex of the Drinfeld upper half space. Note that the filtered
(\varphi,N)
-module in
[
Schr11
]
depends on some non-canonical choices of normalization of parameters, and it is à priori unclear which choice gives the correct
\rho
that “corresponds to”
V_{W_{\alpha_{1}},W_{\alpha_{2}},W_{\alpha_{3}}}
under
p
-adic Langlands correspondence.
We slightly reformulate Schraen’s result by replacing the three
E
-lines
W_{\alpha_{1}}
,
W_{\alpha_{2}}
and
W_{\alpha_{3}}
by a single hyperplane
W\subseteq\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\emptyset}
which is the preimage of
W_{\alpha_{3}}
under

(1.11)

\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\emptyset}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\emptyset}/\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\{\alpha_{i}\}}\cup W_{\alpha_{i}}\right)\cong\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{2}(v_{\Delta}^{\rm{an}},V_{W_{\alpha_{1}},W_{\alpha_{2}}}).

It is clear that the hyperplane
W\subseteq\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\emptyset}
is determined by
W_{\alpha_{1}}
,
W_{\alpha_{2}}
and
W_{\alpha_{3}}
. Conversely,
W_{\alpha_{3}}
is the image of
W
under (
1.11
), and
W_{\alpha_{i}}
is uniquely characterized by the equality

(1.12)

E\mathrm{val}_{\alpha_{3-i}}\cup W_{\alpha_{i}}=W\cap(E\mathrm{val}_{\alpha_{3-i}}\cup\mathbf{E}_{\{\alpha_{i}\},\emptyset})

for each
i=1,2
. In other words, we simplify Schraen’s definition of
\mathscr{L}
-invariants by gluing
W_{\alpha_{1}}
,
W_{\alpha_{2}}
and
W_{\alpha_{3}}
together to a single hyperplane
W\subseteq\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\emptyset}
.

In general, as
\mathbf{E}_{\{\alpha\},\emptyset}\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\rm{cont}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},E)
contains a canonical
E
-line
E\mathrm{val}_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha\in I
, we obtain a canonical
E
-line
\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}^{\infty}\cong\mathbf{E}_{I\setminus I^{\prime},\emptyset}^{\infty}
by cup product of
E\mathrm{val}_{\alpha}
coming from each
\alpha\in I\setminus I^{\prime}
. Hence, we can define
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{I,I^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\emptyset}
as the image of

(1.13)

\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,I}^{\infty}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{I^{\prime},\emptyset}^{\infty}\xrightarrow{\cup}\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\emptyset}.

We are therefore lead to the following simple definition of Breuil-Schraen
\mathscr{L}
-invariants for general
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
, based on Theorem
1.1

Definition 1.3
.

A
Breuil-Schraen
\mathscr{L}
-invariant
for
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
is a hyperplane
W\subseteq\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\emptyset}
such that

•

W\cap\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{I,I^{\prime}}\subsetneq W
for each
I^{\prime}\subseteq I\subseteq\Delta
(and we write
W_{I,I^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}
for the unique hyperplane such that
\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,I}^{\infty}\otimes W_{I,I^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{I^{\prime},\emptyset}^{\infty}
is the preimage of
W
under (
1.13
));

•

(
1.3
) induces an isomorphism

\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}/W_{I,I^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{I^{\prime},I^{\prime\prime}}/W_{I^{\prime},I^{\prime\prime}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime\prime}}/W_{I,I^{\prime\prime}}

for each
I^{\prime\prime}\subseteq I^{\prime}\subseteq I\subseteq\Delta
.

As the first (resp. the second) condition is an open condition (resp. a closed condition), the moduli space of Breuil-Schraen
\mathscr{L}
-invariants is given by an explicit locally closed subscheme of the projective space
\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\emptyset})
. We prove in Theorem
5.9
that this moduli space is non-canonically isomorphic to
U^{+}
, the unipotent radical of
B^{+}
. Note that
U^{+}
is naturally isomorphic (via Fontaine’s
\mathbf{D}_{\rm{st}}
) to the moduli space of
\rho
of the form (
1.1
) that satisfies
N^{n-1}\neq 0
. Compared to the approach of
[
Schr11
]
, our definition of
\mathscr{L}
-invariants has the advantage of being simple and independent of choices of bases of various
\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}
. When
n=3
, the first condition reduces to
W_{\alpha_{1}}\neq E\mathrm{val}\neq W_{\alpha_{2}}
and (
1.9
), and the second condition reduces to (
1.12
).

We conjecture that, for each Breuil-Schraen
\mathscr{L}
-invariant
W\subseteq\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\emptyset}
, one should be able to define

•

a locally analytic representation
\mathcal{W}
of
\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
with
\Dim_{E}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\mathrm{St}_{n}^{\rm{an}},\mathcal{W})=1
and any embedding
\mathrm{St}_{n}^{\rm{an}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{W}
induces a surjection

\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\emptyset}=\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{n-1}(1_{n},\mathrm{St}_{n}^{\rm{an}})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{n-1}(1_{n},\mathcal{W})

with kernel
W
;

•

a unique semi-stable
\rho_{W}:\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n}(E)
such that
\mathcal{W}
embeds into the conjectural representation
\Pi(\rho_{W})
given by
p
-adic Langlands correspondence.

Remark 1.4
.

The existence of such
\mathcal{W}
and
\rho_{W}
is known for
n\leq 3
thanks to the following work.

•

When
n=2
, we can simply take
\mathcal{W}
to be
V_{W}
as in (
1.5
), which was firstly introduced by Breuil in
[
Bre04
]
(see the paragraph in
loc.it.
before Conjecture 1.1.1) as
\Sigma(2,\mathcal{L})
(with
\mathcal{L}\in E
explicitly determined by
W
). Let
f
be a cusp new form of weight
2
whose attached Galois representation is
\rho_{f}
. Assume that
\rho_{f,p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\rho_{f}|_{\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})}
is semi-stable non-crystalline. In Corollaire 1.1.6 of
[
Bre10
]
, Breuil proves that (up to an unramified twist)
\Sigma(2,\mathcal{L})
(or rather its completion
B(2,\mathcal{L})
) embeds into the
f
-isotypic Hecke eigenspace inside the completed cohomology of a tower of modular curves, if and only if the equality
\mathscr{L}(f)=\mathcal{L}
holds with
\mathscr{L}(f)
being the Fontaine-Mazur
\mathscr{L}
-invariant attached to
\rho_{f,p}
. In other words,
\mathscr{L}(f)
is determined by
W
and we can take
\rho_{W}=\rho_{f,p}
. Note that we can also take
\mathcal{W}
to be another uniserial locally analytic representation
V_{W}^{+}
of length four that fits into the short exact seqnece

V_{W}\hookrightarrow V_{W}^{+}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{I}(s\cdot 0)

where
\widetilde{I}(s\cdot 0)
is another irreducible locally analytic principal series (cf. the paragraph before Lemma 3.13 of
[
BD20
]
). In terms of terminology of
[
BD20
]
, our
V_{W}
(resp.
V_{W}^{+}
) is isomorphic to
\pi(0,\psi)^{-}
(resp.
\pi(0,\psi)
) with
\psi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\rm{cont}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},E)
satisfying
W=E\psi
(see (3.23) and (3.27) of
loc.it.
). According to
[
Col10
]
and Theorem 1.2.1 of
[
Eme11
]
, the locally analytic representation
\Pi(\rho_{W})
attached to
\rho_{W}
via
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence is actually isomorphic to
V_{W}^{+}
. Therefore
V_{W}
and
V_{W}^{+}
are the only two possible choices of
\mathcal{W}
here.

•

When
n=3
, following his
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}
-conjecture in
[
Bre19
]
, Breuil constructs an explicit family of locally analytic representations (Théorème 1.2 of
loc.it.
) and proves a weaker version of local-global compatibility (Théorème 1.3 of
loc.it.
) for them, compared with the strong version in Conjecture 1.4 of
loc.it.
. We write
D
for the rank three
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module (over Robba ring) associated with an ordinary Galois representation
\rho
of the form (
1.1
) with
n=3
. In
[
BD20
]
, Breuil-Ding attach to
D
two locally analytic representations
\Pi(D)^{-}\subseteq\Pi(D)
of
\mathrm{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
(see (3.111) and (3.112) of
loc.it.
). Then they prove Conjecture 1.4 of
[
Bre19
]
(see Theorem 1.1 of
[
BD20
]
) crucially using the
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. We write
\widetilde{\Pi}(D)^{-}
(resp.
\widetilde{\Pi}(D)
) for the amalgamate sum of
\Pi(D)^{-}
(resp.
\Pi(D)
) and
\mathrm{St}_{3}^{\rm{an}}
over the length five subrepresentation with Jordan–Hölder factors
\mathrm{St}_{3}^{\infty}
,
C_{1,1}
,
C_{2,1}
,
\widetilde{C}_{1,2}
,
\widetilde{C}_{2,2}
(see (3.111) of
loc.it.
for the illustration of this subrepresentation with each factor defined in paragraphs before it). Then we can combine Proposition 6.8, Theorem 7.1 of
[
Qian21
]
with Theorem 1.1 of
[
BD20
]
and obtain

(1.14)

\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\emptyset}=\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{2}(1_{3},\mathrm{St}_{3}^{\rm{an}})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{2}(1_{3},\widetilde{\Pi}^{-}(D))\cong\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}^{2}(1_{3},\widetilde{\Pi}(D))

with the last two terms being one dimensional. Consequently, if we write
W
for the kernel of (
1.14
), then we can let
\mathcal{W}
be either
\widetilde{\Pi}(D)^{-}
or
\widetilde{\Pi}(D)
, and
\rho_{W}
be the
\rho
that defines
D
. The minimal possible choice of
\mathcal{W}
(under inclusion between representations) is actually

\mathrm{ker}(\widetilde{\Pi}(D)^{-}\twoheadrightarrow v_{\{\alpha\}}^{\infty})

for any
\alpha\in\Delta
, and in particular is not unique. On the other hand, under further restriction on
\mathcal{W}
specified in Conjecture
5.11
, it is possible that there exists a unique maximal choice of
\mathcal{W}
.

We refer the conjectural realization of
\rho_{W}
inside Drinfeld upper half space to Conjecture
5.18
.

Remark 1.5
.

It is natural to ask if Theorem
1.1
admits natural generalization to other reductive groups (with
v_{I}^{\rm{an}}
defined exactly the same way for each
I\subseteq\Delta
). Indeed, most combinatorial construction in this paper works for more general reductive groups and many
\mathrm{Ext}
-groups between
v_{I}^{\rm{an}}
can be computed. However, for general group, it seems unclear how to extend the definition of Breuil-Schraen
\mathscr{L}
-invariants using cup products involving only
\mathrm{Ext}
-groups between
v_{I}^{\rm{an}}
.

Remark 1.6
.

The space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\rm{cont}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times},E)
contains a unique canonical
E
-line which is
E\mathrm{val}
, so
\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}
contains a unique canonical
E
-line
E\mathrm{val}_{\alpha}
whenever
I\setminus I^{\prime}=\{\alpha\}
. For general
I^{\prime}\subseteq I\subseteq\Delta
, there exists a minimal lattice of
canonical subspaces
of
\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}
generated by the image of the cup product of various
\mathbf{E}_{I^{\prime\prime},I^{\prime\prime\prime}}
(for some choices of
I^{\prime}\subseteq I^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq I^{\prime\prime}\subseteq I
) with various
E\mathrm{val}_{\alpha}
satisfying
\alpha\in I\setminus I^{\prime}
. There exists an even more interesting filtration on
\mathbf{E}_{I,I^{\prime}}
induced from the layer structure of
v_{I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}
as a finite length admissible locally analytic representation. We will study this second filtration as well as its relative position with respect to the aforementioned lattice of canonical subspaces in a forthcoming work, which should lead to a proof of Conjecture
5.18
which generalizes Théorème 6.23 and Remarque 6.24 of
[
Schr11
]
. These computations also shed light on the construction (in the
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
case) of locally analytic representations in Breuil’s
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}
conjecture as well as those in Theorem 1.1 of
[
Qian21
]
.

1.2.
Organization of the paper

In Section
2
, we systematically compute various locally analytic cohomology groups of locally analytic generalized Steinberg representations (LAGS for short). As each LAGS is derived equivalent to a complex of (direct sum of) parabolically induced principal series, we reduce our problem to the computation of a spectral sequence called
E_{\bullet,I_{0},I_{1}}^{\bullet,\bullet}
(see Section
2.2
). The first page
E_{1,I_{0},I_{1}}^{\bullet,\bullet}
can be easily written down with an explicit basis using results from Section
2.1
, so our main task is to explicitly compute the second page
E_{2,I_{0},I_{1}}^{\bullet,\bullet}
and show that the spectral sequence actually degenerates at its second page. To achieve this, we construct an explicit (and much smaller) combinatorial subcomplex
E_{1,I_{0},I_{1},\diamond}^{\bullet,k}
of
E_{1,I_{0},I_{1}}^{\bullet,k}
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, based on the notion of
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic tuples
(see Definition
2.13
). The key technical ingredient is to show that the
E_{1,I_{0},I_{1},\diamond}^{\bullet,k}\rightarrow E_{1,I_{0},I_{1}}^{\bullet,k}
is actually a quasi-isomorphism (see Proposition
2.23
), which reduces our problem to the computation of cohomology of the complex
E_{1,I_{0},I_{1},\diamond}^{\bullet,k}
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. In Section
2.5
, we complete the proof of Theorem
2.28
which is the main result of Section
2
. In Section
2.6
, we consider a variant of
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic tuples which will be crucially used in Section
4.3
.

In Section
3
, we prove a decomposition theorem for certain space of locally analytic distributions in Proposition
3.9
, which is technically fundamental for computation of
N
-homology (with
N
being unipotent radical of some parabolic subgroup) of locally analytic representations using Bruhat decomposition. This generalizes results from Section 4.5 of
[
Schr11
]
.

In Section
4
, we compute various (higher)
\mathrm{Ext}
-groups between LAGS. Using results from Section
3
, we prove a generalization of Bernstein–Zelevinsky geometric lemma (see Section 2.12 of
[
BZ77
]
) to locally analytic setting, and in particular prove some vanishing results on
N
-(co)homology in Lemma
4.14
and Lemma
4.15
. The main result of Section
4.1
is Proposition
4.16
, which computes all (higher)
\mathrm{Ext}
-groups between all
i_{I}^{\rm{an}}
. Proposition
4.16
allows us to compute
\mathrm{Ext}
-groups between LAGS by a spectral sequence, which turns out to be among the ones already treated in Section
2
, so Theorem
4.22
easily follows. Section
4.3
is devoted to explicit computation of the cup product map using the commutative diagram (
4.20
) and (
4.23
), which ends up with Theorem
4.31
.

In Section
5
, we define and study what we call
Breuil-Schraen
\mathscr{L}
-invariants
. In particular, we propose Conjecture
5.11
that relate them to Breuil-Ding’s approach of higher
\mathscr{L}
-invariants, and propose Conjecture
5.18
which generalizes
[
Schr11
]
on potential realization of
\mathscr{L}
-invariants inside the de Rham complex of Drinfeld upper half spaces. Some expectation on this realization as well as its relation to other objects are sketched in Remark
5.19
.

[
Schr11
]
on
\mathrm{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
case, and this paper is the first step towards full generalization of
[
Schr11
]
to
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
. We would like to thank Yiwen Ding for his papers with Breuil and extensive discussions around higher
\mathscr{L}
-invariants and to thank Florian Herzig for his constant interest and regular communication. We thank Weibo Fu, Yongquan Hu, Ma Luo, Liang Xiao, Daxin Xu and Lizao Ye for many useful discussions. Finally, we would like to thank Lennart Gehrmann for discussion around
[
Geh21
]
, which together with
[
Schr11
]
makes it clear that
\mathrm{Ext}
-groups such as
\mathbf{E}_{\Delta,\emptyset}
are crucial to the study of (higher)
\mathscr{L}
-invariants. This work is mainly done when the author was a Posdoctoral Fellow at University of Toronto, and the author would like to thank them for their hospitality.

1.4.
Notation and preliminary

Throughout this paper,
p
is prime number and
K
is a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. We fix another
p
-adic field
E
as well as an embedding
E\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
such that each embedding
\iota:K\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
factors through it. Note that we write
S
for the set of all embeddings
\iota:K\hookrightarrow E
and identify it with the set of all embeddings
K\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
via the fixed embedding
E\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.

We recall some standard facts on (admissible) locally analytic representations of
p
-adic analytic groups.
Let
G
be a
p
-adic analytic group and
D(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}D(G,E)
be the space of locally analytic distributions on
G
(see
[
ST03
]
for its definition).
We write
\mathrm{Mod}_{D(G)}
for the abelian category of (abstract)
D(G)
-modules, and
\mathcal{M}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}D(\mathrm{Mod}_{D(G)})
for its derived category. Given two bounded strict chain complexes of admissible (
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-)locally analytic representations
\mathbf{C}_{1}
,
\mathbf{C}_{2}
of
G
, we consider their strong dual
\mathbf{C}_{1}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{C}_{2}^{\prime}
, and then define the locally analytic
\mathrm{Ext}
-groups

\mathrm{Ext}^{\bullet}_{G}(\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\mathrm{Ext}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{M}(G)}(\mathbf{C}_{2}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}_{1}^{\prime})

by
\mathrm{Ext}
in the derived category
\mathcal{M}(G)
. In particular, we use the notation

H^{\bullet}(G,\Pi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\mathrm{Ext}^{\bullet}_{G}(1,\Pi)

for each admissible locally analytic representation
\Pi
of
G
.

Let
\mathbf{C}_{1}=[\mathbf{C}_{1}^{\ell}]_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}},\mathbf{C}_{2}=[\mathbf{C}_{2}^{\ell}]_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}
be two bounded strict chain complexes of admissible locally analytic representations, and we abuse
\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2}
for the corresponding objects in
\mathcal{M}(G)
. For each
k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can consider
\mathrm{Ext}^{k}_{G}(\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2}^{\ell})
as well as
\mathrm{Ext}^{k}_{G}(\mathbf{C}_{1}^{\ell},\mathbf{C}_{2})
.

Lemma 1.7
.

(i)

There exists a spectral sequence converge to
\mathrm{Ext}^{\bullet}_{G}(\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2})
whose first page has
k
-th row given by

\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{k}_{G}(\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2}^{\ell})\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{k}_{G}(\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2}^{\ell+1})\rightarrow\cdots.

(ii)

There exists a spectral sequence converge to
\mathrm{Ext}^{\bullet}_{G}(\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2})
whose first page has
k
-th row given by

\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{k}_{G}(\mathbf{C}_{1}^{\ell},\mathbf{C}_{2})\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{k}_{G}(\mathbf{C}_{1}^{\ell-1},\mathbf{C}_{2})\rightarrow\cdots.

Proof.

This is standard inside the derived category
\mathcal{M}(G)
.
∎

Now assume that
G
is the set of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-points of a
p
-adic reductive group, and
P\subseteq G
is a parabolic subgroup with unipotent radical
N_{P}
and Levi quotient
L
.

Lemma 1.8
.

Let
M\in\mathrm{Mod}_{D(G)}
and
M_{L}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{D(L)}
with
M_{L}
satisfying the (FIN) condition in Section 6 of
[
ST05
]
. Then we have the following spectral sequence

\mathrm{Ext}_{D(L)}^{k_{1}}(M_{L},H^{k_{2}}(N_{P},M))\Rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{D(G)}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(D(G)\otimes_{D(P)}M_{L},M).

Proof.

This is equation (43) of
[
Bre19
]
.
∎

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer. We set
G_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\mathrm{GL}_{n/K}
and let
B_{n}
(resp.
B_{n}^{+}
, resp.
T_{n}
) be its lower triangular Borel subgroup (resp. its upper triangular Borel subgroup, resp. its diagonal maximal torus). We consider the set
\Delta_{n}
of positive simple roots and fix a bijection
\Delta_{n}\cong\{1,2,\dots,n-1\}
which sends
(i,i+1)
to
i
for each
1\leq i\leq n-1
.
There is a natural bijection between the set of subsets
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
and parabolic subgroups
P_{n,I}\subseteq G_{n}
containing
B_{n}
(with
P_{n,\Delta_{n}}=G_{n}
and
P_{n,\emptyset}=B_{n}
). We write
L_{n,I}\subseteq P_{n,I}
for the standard Levi subgroup. Let
Z_{n}
be the center of
G_{n}
and
Z_{n,I}
be the center of
L_{n,I}
. We set

\overline{G}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}G_{n}/Z_{n}
,
\overline{L}_{n,I}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}L_{n,I}/Z_{n}
and
\overline{Z}_{n,I}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}Z_{n,I}/Z_{n}

for each
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
. We use the notation
\mathfrak{g}_{n}
(resp.
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n}
, resp.
\mathfrak{l}_{n,I}
, resp.
\overline{\mathfrak{l}}_{n,I}
) for the Lie algebra associated with
G_{n}
(resp.
\overline{G}_{n}
, resp.
L_{n,I}
, resp.
\overline{L}_{n,I}
).

Throughout this paper, we abuse the notation for group schemes (resp. Lie algebras) for their set of
K
-points, and therefore view them as
p
-adic Lie groups (resp.
p
-adic Lie algebras). For a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
over
K
, we set
\mathfrak{g}_{E,\iota}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{K,\iota}E
for each embedding
\iota:K\hookrightarrow E
, and note that
\mathfrak{g}_{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}E\cong\prod_{\iota:K\hookrightarrow E}\mathfrak{g}_{E,\iota}
. We write
G_{n,E}
for the base change of
\mathrm{Res}_{K/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G_{n}
to
E
, and similarly for other groups above.

For each
p
-adic reductive group
G
containing a parabolic subgroup
P
, we write
(\mathrm{Ind}_{P}^{G}\cdot)^{\rm{an}}
(resp.
(\mathrm{Ind}_{P}^{G}\cdot)^{\infty}
) for the locally analytic (resp. smooth) parabolic induction functor.
For each pair of subsets
I\subseteq I^{\prime}\subseteq\Delta_{n}
, we set

i_{n,I,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(\pi_{I})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\left(\mathrm{Ind}_{P_{n,I}\cap L_{n,I^{\prime}}}^{L_{n,I^{\prime}}}\pi_{I}\right)^{\rm{an}}

for each locally analytic representation
\pi_{I}
of
L_{n,I}
and note that
i_{n,I,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(\pi_{I})
is admissible if
\pi_{I}
is. If
\pi_{I}
is furthermore smooth, we define
i_{n,I,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{\infty}}(\pi_{I})
similarly.

Let
\lambda\in X(T_{n,E})
be a weight which is dominant with respect to
B_{n,E}^{+}
. For each
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
, we write
F_{n,I}(\lambda)
for the algebraic representation of
L_{n,I,E}
with highest weight
\lambda
(with respect to
B_{n,E}^{+}
), which induces a finite dimensional
E
-representation of the
p
-adic Lie group
L_{n,I}
.
We define
i_{n,I}^{\infty}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}i_{n,I,\Delta_{n}}^{\infty}(1_{\overline{L}_{n,I}})
and a
smooth generalized Steinberg representation

V_{n,I}^{\infty}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}i_{n,I}^{\infty}/\sum_{I\subsetneq I^{\prime}\subseteq\Delta_{n}}i_{n,I^{\prime}}^{\infty}
for each
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
. We define
i_{n,I}^{\rm{alg}}(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}F_{n,\Delta_{n}}(\lambda)\otimes_{E}i_{n,I}^{\infty}
and the
locally algebraic generalized Steinberg representation

V_{n,I}^{\rm{alg}}(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}F_{n,\Delta_{n}}(\lambda)\otimes_{E}V_{n,I}^{\infty}
. For each
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
, we know that
V_{n,I}^{\infty}
and thus
V_{n,I}^{\rm{alg}}(\lambda)
is irreducible. We write
\mathrm{Ext}_{G_{n},\lambda}^{\bullet}
for the
\mathrm{Ext}
-groups fixing the central character which equals that of
F_{n,\Delta_{n}}(\lambda)
(see Remarque 5.1.3 of
[
Bre19
]
for similar notation).

Similarly, we define

(1.15)

V_{n,I,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}i_{n,I,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(F_{n,I}(\lambda))/\sum_{I\subsetneq I^{\prime\prime}\subseteq I^{\prime}}i_{n,I,I^{\prime\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(F_{n,I^{\prime\prime}}(\lambda))

for each
I\subseteq I^{\prime}\subseteq\Delta_{n}
.
In particular, we have
i_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}i_{n,I,\Delta_{n}}^{\rm{an}}(F_{n,I}(\lambda))
and a
locally analytic generalized Steinberg representation

V_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}V_{n,I,\Delta_{n}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)
for each
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
with
\mathrm{St}_{n}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}V_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)
. It is clear that
V_{n,\Delta_{n}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)\cong F_{n,\Delta_{n}}(\lambda)
.

We have a natural embedding
i_{n,I}^{\rm{alg}}(\lambda)\hookrightarrow i_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)
, which induces an embedding
V_{n,I}^{\rm{alg}}(\lambda)\hookrightarrow V_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)
that identifies
V_{n,I}^{\rm{alg}}(\lambda)
with the locally algebraic vectors of
V_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)
(as we know the set of Jordan–Hölder factors (with multiplicity) of
V_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)
thanks to
[
OS13
]
and
[
OS15
]
). We emphasize that, for each
I^{\prime}\subseteq I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
, we have a distinguished embedding
\kappa_{I,I^{\prime}}^{\infty}:i_{n,I}^{\infty}\hookrightarrow i_{n,I^{\prime}}^{\infty}
, (resp.
\kappa_{I,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{alg}}(\lambda):i_{n,I}^{\rm{alg}}(\lambda)\hookrightarrow i_{n,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{alg}}(\lambda)
, resp.
\kappa_{I,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda):i_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)\hookrightarrow i_{n,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)
) that sends various locally constant (resp. locally algebraic, resp. locally analytic) functions on
\overline{G}_{n}
to themselves (concerning the definition of parabolic induction).

2.
Cohomology of generalized Steinberg

In this section, we compute cohomologies of locally analytic generalized Steinberg representations via a careful combinatorial study of the first and second pages of a spectral sequence
E_{\bullet,I_{0},I_{1}}^{\bullet,\bullet}
(for some
I_{0}\subseteq I_{1}\subseteq\Delta_{n}
) induced from the locally analytic Tits complex
\mathbf{C}_{I_{0},I_{1}}(\lambda)
(see (
2.10
) for its definition and
[
OS13
]
for this terminology). The key combinatorial notion is that of
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic tuples (see Definition
2.13
), and the main results of this section are Proposition
2.23
and Theorem
2.28
.

2.1.
Standard bases of group cohomology

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a (split) reductive lie algebra. It follows from Théorème 9.3 of
[
Kos50
]
that we have an isomorphism of graded algebra

H_{\ast}(\mathfrak{g},1)\cong(\wedge^{\ast}\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{g}}.

Let
D(\mathfrak{g})
be the subspace of
(\wedge^{\ast}\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{g}}
spanned by elements of the form
u\wedge v
(namely decomposable). Note that we have a pairing
H_{\ast}(\mathfrak{g},1)\times H^{\ast}(\mathfrak{g},1)
and let
P(\mathfrak{g})^{\prime}
be the subspace of
H^{\ast}(\mathfrak{g},1)
given by the orthogonal complement of
D(\mathfrak{g})
. We set
P^{k}(\mathfrak{g})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}P(\mathfrak{g})^{\prime}\cap H^{k}(\mathfrak{g},1)
for each
k\geq 0
.

Theorem 2.1

(Koszul,
[
Kos50
]
)
.

We have an isomorphism of graded algebra

(2.1)

\wedge^{\ast}(P(\mathfrak{g})^{\prime})\cong H^{\ast}(\mathfrak{g},1_{\mathfrak{g}}).

Moreover, (
2.1
) is functorial in the following sense: for each morphism of (split) reductive lie algebra
\varphi:~\mathfrak{h}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
, we have an induced morphism
\varphi^{\ast}:~P(\mathfrak{g})^{\prime}\rightarrow P(\mathfrak{h})^{\prime}
which determines the morphism
\varphi^{\ast}:~H^{\ast}(\mathfrak{g},1_{\mathfrak{g}})\rightarrow H^{\ast}(\mathfrak{h},1_{\mathfrak{h}})
completely, via (
2.1
).

The following classical theorem reduces various group cohomologies to Lie algebra cohomologies.

Theorem 2.2

(Casselman–Wigner,
[
CW74
]
)
.

The canonical morphism

H^{\ast}(G,1_{G})\rightarrow H^{\ast}(\mathfrak{g},1_{\mathfrak{g}})

is an isomorphism of graded algebra if
G
is the set of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-points of a semisimple group over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

Note that we do not require the semisimple group over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
in Theorem
2.2
to be split. Also note that similar results hold for compact
p
-adic analytic groups due to
[
La65
]
.

We recall
K
,
E
,
S=\{\iota:K\hookrightarrow E\}
and other notation from
1.4
.
For each
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
, we set
r_{I}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}n-\#I
and there exists a partition
n_{I}=(n_{d})_{1\leq d\leq r_{I}}
of
n
such that we have a decomposition into Levi blocks

L_{n,I}\cong G_{n_{1}}\times G_{n_{2}}\times\cdots\times G_{n_{r_{I}}}

which induces

(2.2)

\overline{L}_{n,I}\cong\overline{G}_{n_{1}}\times\overline{G}_{n_{2}}\times\cdots\times\overline{G}_{n_{r_{I}}}\times\overline{Z}_{n,I}.

with
\overline{Z}_{n,I}\cong(K^{\times})^{r_{I}-1}
.

For each
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
and each
1\leq d\leq r_{I}
, we have an embedding

(2.3)

\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n_{d}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathfrak{l}}_{n,I}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n}

which induces an embedding
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n_{d},E}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E}
and therefore a morphism

(2.4)

\mathrm{Res}_{n,I}^{k,d}:~P^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E})\rightarrow P^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n_{d},E}).

The embedding
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n_{d},E}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E}
is the direct sum of
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n_{d},E,\iota}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E,\iota}
for each
\iota\in S
, and thus (
2.4
) decomposes into direct sum of

\mathrm{Res}_{n,I,\iota}^{k,d}:~P^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E,\iota})\rightarrow P^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n_{d},E,\iota})

for all
\iota\in S
.

The following result on primitive classes for Lie algebra cohomology is well-known.

Theorem 2.3
.

For each
n\geq 2
and
\iota\in S
, we have
\dim_{E}P^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E,\iota})=1
if
k=2m-1
for some
2\leq m\leq n
and
P^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E,\iota})=0
otherwise.
Moreover, the morphism
\mathrm{Res}_{n,I,\iota}^{k,d}
is an isomorphism if
k=2m-1
for some
2\leq m\leq n_{d}
and is zero otherwise.

Lemma 2.4
.

The map
\mathrm{Res}_{n,I,\iota}^{k,d}
depends only on
n
,
k
,
n_{d}
and
\iota
.

Proof.

We fix
n
,
n^{\prime}\leq n
,
3\leq k\leq 2n^{\prime}-1
, and
\iota\in S
throughout the proof. Let
f_{1}
be the embedding

\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n^{\prime},E}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathfrak{l}}_{n,I,E}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E}

when
I=\{1,\dots,n^{\prime}-1\}
and
d=1
. We consider the adjoint action of
\overline{G}_{n}
on
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E}
and set
f_{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\mathrm{Ad}(g)(f_{1}):\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n^{\prime},E}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E}
. For each
g\in\overline{G}_{n}
,
f_{g}
induces a map
f_{g}^{\ast}:P^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E,\iota})\rightarrow P^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n^{\prime},E,\iota})
which is an isomorphism between one dimensional spaces thanks to Theorem
2.3
. Consequently, there exists a unique morphism
\chi:\overline{G}_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}
such that
f_{g}^{\ast}=\chi(g)f_{1}^{\ast}
for each
g\in\overline{G}_{n}
. As
\overline{G}_{n}
is adjoint,
\chi
must be constant and thus
f_{g}^{\ast}=f_{1}^{\ast}
for each
g\in\overline{G}_{n}
. We finally notice that the map
\mathrm{Res}_{n,I,\iota}^{k,d}
constructed from different choices of
I
(with the same
n
,
k
,
n_{d}=n^{\prime}
and
\iota
) are just
f_{g}^{\ast}
for different choices of
g
, and therefore are all equal.
∎

For each
n\geq 2
,
k\geq 3
and
\iota\in S
satisfying
P^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E,\iota})\neq 0
, we choose a basis
\{v_{n,\iota}^{k}\}
of
P^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E,\iota})
such that they are compatible under all morphisms of the form
\mathrm{Res}_{n,I}^{k,d}
. For technical convenience, we set
v_{n}^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}1\in H^{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{n},1_{\mathfrak{g}_{n}})
for each
n\geq 1
. For each
n\geq 2
and
k\geq 3
, we define
\Sigma_{n,k}
as the set of subsets

\Lambda=\{(m_{1},\iota_{1}),\dots,(m_{r},\iota_{r})\}\subseteq\{3,5,\dots,2n-3,2n-1\}\times S

for some
r\geq 1
such that
\sum_{s=1}^{r}m_{s}=k
. We define
P^{\Lambda}_{n}
as the one dimensional subspace of
\wedge^{\ast}\left(P(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E})^{\prime}\right)
given by

P^{m_{1}}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E,\iota_{1}})\wedge\cdots\wedge P^{m_{r}}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E,\iota_{r}})

for each
\Lambda\in\Sigma_{n,k}
, which clearly does not depend on the choice of order on
\Lambda
. For each
\Lambda\in\Sigma_{n,k}
, we write
\widetilde{\Lambda}
for an enhancement of
\Lambda
to an ordered set
\{(m_{1},\iota_{1}),\cdots,(m_{r},\iota_{r})\}
, and then set
v_{n}^{\widetilde{\Lambda}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}v_{n,\iota_{1}}^{m_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n,\iota_{r}}^{m_{r}}
. So different
v_{n}^{\widetilde{\Lambda}}
for the same
\Lambda
differ by an explicit sign. For each
n\geq 1
, we also set
\Sigma_{n,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\{\emptyset\}
and
v_{n}^{\emptyset}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}v_{n}^{0}=1\in H^{0}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E},1_{\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E}})
for later convenience.

Corollary 2.5
.

We have canonical isomorphisms

(2.5)

H^{k}(\overline{G}_{n},1_{\overline{G}_{n}})\cong H^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n},1_{\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n}})\cong H^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E},1_{\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n,E}})\cong\bigoplus_{\Lambda\in\Sigma_{n,k}}P^{\Lambda}_{n}

for each
k\geq 0
(with
1_{\overline{G}_{n}}
and
1_{\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n}}
understood to be
E
-representation of
\overline{G}_{n}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n}
respectively). In particular,
H^{k}(\overline{G}_{n},1_{\overline{G}_{n}})=H^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n},1_{\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n}})=0
if
k>(n^{2}-1)[K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]
.

Proof.

The first isomorphism follows from Theorem
2.2
. The second isomorphism follows from Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
2.3
. The third isomorphism follows from the Chevalley-Eilenberg complexes that compute the source and the target. The vanishing is clear from
\dim\overline{G}_{n}=\dim\overline{\mathfrak{g}}_{n}=(n^{2}-1)[K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]
, and can also be seen from the fact that

n^{2}-1=\sum_{s=2}^{n}2s-1

which implies that
\Sigma_{n,k}=\emptyset
if
k>(n^{2}-1)[K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]
.
∎

We will use without explanation the isomorphisms in Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
2.2
and view
P^{\Lambda}_{n}
as a subspace of
H^{k}(\overline{G}_{n},1_{\overline{G}_{n}})
from now on.

Lemma 2.6
.

We have a canonical isomorphism

H^{k}(\overline{Z}_{n,I},1_{\overline{Z}_{n,I}})\cong\wedge^{k}(\mathrm{Hom}(\overline{Z}_{n,I},E))

for each
n\geq 1
,
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
and
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

This is standard as
\overline{Z}_{n,I}
is abelian (cf. Corollaire 3.11 of
[
Schr11
]
).
∎

Note that we have an embedding
\overline{Z}_{n,\Delta_{n}\setminus\{i\}}\hookrightarrow\overline{Z}_{n,I}
for each
i\in\Delta_{n}\setminus I
, which actually induces an isomorphism

(2.6)

\overline{Z}_{n,I}\cong\prod_{i\in\Delta_{n}\setminus I}\overline{Z}_{n,\Delta_{n}\setminus\{i\}}.

Let
\mathrm{val}:K^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow E
be the
p
-adic valuation function with
\mathrm{val}(p)=1
. We fix a choice of
p
-adic logarithm
\mathrm{log}:\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\times}\rightarrow E
satisfying
\mathrm{log}(p)=0
and write
\mathrm{log}_{\iota}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\mathrm{log}\circ\iota
for each
\iota\in S
, so that
\{\mathrm{val}\}\sqcup\{\mathrm{log}_{\iota}\mid\iota\in S\}
forms a basis of
\mathrm{Hom}(K^{\times},E)
. Using the standard isomorphism
\overline{Z}_{n,\Delta_{n}\setminus\{i\}}\cong K^{\times}
, we obtain a basis
\mathcal{B}_{n,\Delta_{n}\setminus\{i\}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\{\mathrm{val}_{i}\}\sqcup\{\mathrm{log}_{i,\iota}\mid\iota\in S\}
of
\mathrm{Hom}(\overline{Z}_{n,\Delta_{n}\setminus\{i\}},E)
. Hence, we deduce from (
2.6
) that
\mathcal{B}_{n,I}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\bigsqcup_{i\in\Delta_{n}\setminus I}\mathcal{B}_{n,\Delta_{n}\setminus\{i\}}
forms a basis of
\mathrm{Hom}(\overline{Z}_{n,I},E)
. We fix a total order on
\mathcal{B}_{n,\emptyset}
which induces a total order on
\mathcal{B}_{n,I}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{n,\emptyset}
for each
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
. We write
v\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{n,I}
for a subset, which together with the fixed total order determines a unique element of
\wedge^{k}\mathrm{Hom}(\overline{Z}_{n,I},E)
. Through this way, the set of subsets of
\mathcal{B}_{n,I}
with cardinality
k
naturally corresponds to a basis of
\wedge^{k}\mathrm{Hom}(\overline{Z}_{n,I},E)
. Note that each
v\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{n,I}
determines a unique maximal
I_{v}\subseteq\Delta_{n}
containing
I
such that
v\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{n,I_{v}}
. From now on, we will abuse the notation
v
for both a subset of
\mathcal{B}_{n,I}
and the corresponding element of
\wedge^{\#v}\mathrm{Hom}(\overline{Z}_{n,I},E)
(using the fixed total order on
\mathcal{B}_{n,I}
).

Lemma 2.7
.

The isomorphism (
2.2
) induces an isomorphism

H^{k}(\overline{L}_{n,I},1_{\overline{L}_{n,I}})\cong\bigoplus_{k_{0}+k_{1}+\cdots+k_{r_{I}}=k}\wedge^{k_{0}}(\mathrm{Hom}(\overline{Z}_{n,I},E))\otimes_{E}\bigotimes_{d=1}^{r_{I}}H^{k_{d}}(\overline{G}_{n_{d}},1_{\overline{G}_{n_{d}}}).

Proof.

This is simply Kunneth formula (cf. Théorème 3.10 of
[
Schr11
]
) combined with Lemma
2.6
.
∎

For each
i\in\Delta_{n}
, the embedding
L_{n,\Delta\setminus\{i\}}\subseteq G_{n}
induces a canonical morphism

\mathrm{Res}_{n,i}^{k}:~H^{k}(\overline{G}_{n},1_{\overline{G}_{n}})\rightarrow H^{k}(\overline{L}_{n,\Delta_{n}\setminus\{i\}},1_{\overline{L}_{n,\Delta_{n}\setminus\{i\}}}).

For each
\Lambda\in\Sigma_{n,k}
and
i\in\Delta_{n}
, we write
\mathbf{D}_{n,\Lambda,i}
for those subsets
\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda
satisfying
\max\{m\mid(m,\iota)\in\Lambda^{\prime}\}\leq 2i-1
and
\max\{m\mid(m,\iota)\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}\}\leq 2(n-i)-1
. Note that both
\Lambda^{\prime}
and
\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}
are allowed to be empty.
In the following, we fix a total order on
S
, and then fix the choice of enhancement
\widetilde{\Lambda}
to be the one with decreasing order on integers and satisfying
(m,\iota)<(m^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})
whenever
\iota<\iota^{\prime}
, and write
v_{n}^{\Lambda}
instead of
v_{n}^{\widetilde{\Lambda}}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\Lambda\in\Sigma_{n,k}
be a partition and
\widetilde{\Lambda}
be an enhancement of
\Lambda
. Then
\mathrm{Res}_{n,i}^{k}(P^{\Lambda}_{n})\neq 0
if and only if
\mathbf{D}_{n,\Lambda,i}\neq\emptyset
. Moreover, we have

\mathrm{Res}_{n,i}^{k}(v_{n}^{\Lambda})=\sum_{\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathbf{D}_{n,\Lambda,i}}\varepsilon(\Lambda^{\prime})v_{i}^{\Lambda^{\prime}}\otimes_{E}v_{n-i}^{\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}}.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
2.3
.
∎

More generally, for each
I^{\prime}\subseteq I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
, the embedding
L_{n,I^{\prime}}\subseteq L_{n,I}
induces a canonical morphism

(2.7)

\mathrm{Res}_{n,I,I^{\prime}}^{k}:~H^{k}(\overline{L}_{n,I},1_{\overline{L}_{n,I}})\rightarrow H^{k}(\overline{L}_{n,I^{\prime}},1_{\overline{L}_{n,I^{\prime}}}).

Let
i\in I
and
1\leq d\leq r_{I}
such that
1+\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d-1}n_{d^{\prime}}\leq i\leq\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d}n_{d^{\prime}}
, then
\mathrm{Res}_{n,I,I\setminus\{i\}}^{k}
can be clearly recovered from
\mathrm{Res}_{n_{d},\overline{i}}^{k_{d}}
(with
\overline{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}i-\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d-1}n_{d^{\prime}}
) by tensoring with identity morphisms on cohomologies of other Levi blocks of
L_{n,I}
and that of
\overline{Z}_{n,I}
, and then sum over all decompositions
k=\sum_{d=0}^{r_{I}}k_{d}
as in Lemma
2.7
.

Let
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
be a subset and
\underline{k}=\{k_{d}\}_{0\leq d\leq r_{I}}
be a tuple of non-negative integers satisfying
|\underline{k}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\sum_{d=0}^{r_{I}}k_{d}=k
. We choose an element
\Lambda_{d}\in\Sigma_{n_{d},k_{d}}
for each
1\leq d\leq r_{I}
and then write
\underline{\Lambda}=\{\Lambda_{d}\}_{1\leq d\leq r_{I}}
for the tuple. We set

(2.8)

\Sigma_{n,I,\underline{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\prod_{d=1}^{r_{I}}\Sigma_{n_{d},k_{d}}

and

v_{n,I,\underline{k}}^{\underline{\Lambda}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\bigotimes_{d=1}^{r_{I}}v_{n_{d}}^{\Lambda_{d}}\in\bigotimes_{d=1}^{r_{I}}H^{k_{d}}(\overline{G}_{n_{d}},1_{\overline{G}_{n_{d}}})

for each
\underline{\Lambda}\in\Sigma_{n,I,\underline{k}}
.
It is clear that
\{v_{n,I,\underline{k}}^{\underline{\Lambda}}\}_{\underline{\Lambda}\in\Sigma_{n,I,\underline{k}}}
is a basis of
\bigotimes_{d=1}^{r_{I}}H^{k_{d}}(\overline{G}_{n_{d}},1_{\overline{G}_{n_{d}}})
. Moreover, Lemma
2.7
implies that

(2.9)

\{v\otimes_{E}v_{n,I,\underline{k}}^{\underline{\Lambda}}\mid v\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{n,I},~\#v=k_{0},~\underline{\Lambda}\in\Sigma_{n,I,\underline{k}},~|\underline{k}|=k\}

forms a basis of
H^{k}(\overline{L}_{n,I},1_{\overline{L}_{n,I}})
for each
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
and
k\geq 0
.

2.2.
Standard spectral sequences

For each pair of subsets
I_{0}\subseteq I_{1}\subseteq\Delta_{n}
, we consider the following complex

(2.10)

\mathbf{C}_{I_{0},I_{1}}(\lambda):~i_{n,I_{1}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\bigoplus_{I_{0}\subseteq I^{\prime}\subseteq I_{1},\#I=\ell}i_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow i_{n,I_{0}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)

with
i_{n,I_{0}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)
placed at degree
-\#I_{0}
. Now we specify each map of the complex (
2.10
). Recall that we have a distinguished embedding
\kappa_{I,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda):i_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)\hookrightarrow i_{n,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)
for each
I^{\prime}\subseteq I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
. For each
i\in I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
, we define
m(I,i)
to be the number of
i^{\prime}\in I
satisfying
i^{\prime}<i
. Then the map
i_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)\hookrightarrow i_{n,I\setminus\{i\}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)
in (
2.10
) is given by
(-1)^{m(I,i)}\kappa_{I,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)
, for each
I_{0}\subseteq I\subseteq I_{1}
and
i\in I\setminus I_{0}
. One can easily check that such maps actually define a complex as illustrated by (
2.10
).

Remark 2.9
.

There is an easy way to understand the choice of the sign
(-1)^{m(I,i)}
that appears in the definition of the complex. We assume
I_{0}=\emptyset
,
I_{1}=\Delta_{n}
and
\lambda=0
for simplicity, then each
\kappa_{I,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(0)
restrict to the identity map on the space of constant functions on
\overline{G}_{n}
(as a natural subspace of both
i_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(0)
and
i_{n,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(0)
by definition of parabolic induction). We consider the subcomplex
\mathbf{C}
of
\mathbf{C}_{\emptyset,\Delta_{n}}(0)
consisting of constant functions on
\overline{G}_{n}
, then our choice of sign
(-1)^{m(I,i)}
above guarantees that
\mathbf{C}
is exact. In fact, the exact sequence
\mathbf{C}
is simply the
\#\Delta_{n}=n-1
-th tensor power of the exact sequence
0\rightarrow E\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Id}}E\rightarrow 0
that is supported in degree
[-1,0]
.

For later convenience, we also set
\mathbf{C}_{I_{0},I_{1}}(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}0
if
I_{0}\not\subseteq I_{1}
. If we consider two extra sets
I_{0}^{\prime},I_{1}^{\prime}
satisfying
I_{0}\subseteq I_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq I_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq I_{1}
, then we have a natural commutative diagram by considering truncation of complex

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}_{I_{0},I_{1}}(\lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}_{I_{0}^{\prime},I_{1}}(\lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}_{I_{0},I_{1}^{\prime}}(\lambda)}
\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}_{I_{0}^{\prime},I_{1}^{\prime}}(\lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Note that
\mathbf{C}_{I_{0},I_{1}}(\lambda)\cong i_{n,I_{1},\Delta_{n}}^{\rm{an}}(V_{n,I_{0},I_{1}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda))[-\#I_{0}]
(and in particular
\mathbf{C}_{I_{0},\Delta_{n}}(\lambda)\cong V_{n,I_{0}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)[-\#I_{0}]
) in the derived sense.

It follows from Lemma
1.8
and Theorem 7.1 of
[
Koh11
]
that we have canonical isomorphisms

\mathrm{Ext}_{G_{n},\lambda}^{k}(F_{n,\Delta_{n}}(\lambda),i_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda))\cong\mathrm{Ext}_{L_{n,I},\lambda}^{k}(H_{0}(N_{n,I},F_{n,\Delta_{n}}(\lambda)),F_{n,I}(\lambda))\\
\cong\mathrm{Ext}_{L_{n,I},\lambda}^{k}(F_{n,I}(\lambda),F_{n,I}(\lambda))\cong H^{k}(\overline{L}_{n,I},1_{\overline{L}_{n,I}})

for each
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
.
Moreover, for each
I^{\prime}\subseteq I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
, the map

\mathrm{Ext}_{G_{n},\lambda}^{\bullet}(F_{n,\Delta_{n}}(\lambda),i_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda))\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{G_{n},\lambda}^{\bullet}(F_{n,\Delta_{n}}(\lambda),i_{n,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda))

induced from
\kappa_{I,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda):i_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)\hookrightarrow i_{n,I^{\prime}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda)
is simply the restriction map (
2.7
).
It follows from item
(i)
of Lemma
1.7
that there exists a standard spectral sequence
\{E_{r,I_{0},I_{1}}^{-\ell,k}\}_{r\geq 0,\#I_{0}\leq\ell\leq\#I_{1},k\geq 0}
which converges to

\mathrm{Ext}_{G_{n},\lambda}^{\bullet}(F_{n,\Delta_{n}}(\lambda),\mathbf{C}_{I_{0},I_{1}}(\lambda))\cong\mathrm{Ext}_{G_{n},\lambda}^{\bullet+\#I_{0}}(F_{n,\Delta_{n}}(\lambda),i_{n,I_{1},\Delta_{n}}^{\rm{an}}(V_{n,I_{0},I_{1}}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda))).

For each
k\geq 0
and
\#I_{0}\leq\ell\leq n-1
, we have

E_{1,I_{0},I_{1}}^{-\ell,k}\cong\bigoplus_{I_{0}\subseteq I\subseteq I_{1},\#I=\ell}\mathrm{Ext}_{G_{n},\lambda}^{k}(F_{n,\Delta_{n}}(\lambda),i_{n,I}^{\rm{an}}(\lambda))\cong\bigoplus_{I_{0}\subseteq I\subseteq I_{1},\#I=\ell}H^{k}(\overline{L}_{n,I},1_{\overline{L}_{n,I}}),

and the differential
d_{1,I_{0},I_{1}}^{-\ell,k}:~E_{1,I_{0},I_{1}}^{-\ell,k}\rightarrow E_{1,I_{0},I_{1}}^{-\ell+1,k}
has the form

\bigoplus_{i\in I\setminus I_{0}}(-1)^{m(I,i)}\mathrm{Res}_{n,I,I\setminus\{i\}}^{k}

after restricting to
H^{k}(\overline{L}_{n,I},1_{\overline{L}_{n,I}})
, for each
I_{0}\subseteq I\subseteq I_{1}
satisfying
\#I=\ell
.

Given two pair of subsets
I_{0}\subseteq I_{1}\subseteq\Delta_{n}
and
I_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq I_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq\Delta_{n}
, if
I_{0}\subseteq I_{0}^{\prime}
and
I_{1}\subseteq I_{1}^{\prime}
, then the natural map
\mathbf{C}_{I_{0},I_{1}}(\lambda)\rightarrow\mathbf{C}_{I_{0}^{\prime},I_{1}^{\prime}}(\lambda)
induces a map between spectral sequences
E_{\bullet,I_{0},I_{1}}^{\bullet,\bullet}\rightarrow E_{\bullet,I_{0}^{\prime},I_{1}^{\prime}}^{\bullet,\bullet}
.

2.3.
(I_{0},I_{1})
-standard and
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic tuples

In this section, we introduce
(I_{0},I_{1})
-standard and
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic elements (see Definition
2.13
) of the bases introduced in Section
2.1
. These combinatorial notion will be crucial later for the proof of Theorem
2.28
.

Definition 2.10
.

We say that a subset
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
is an
interval
if there exists
1\leq i\leq j\leq n-1
such that
I=\{i,i+1,\dots,j\}
.
For each
I\subseteq\Delta_{n}
, there exists a unique decomposition
I=\bigsqcup_{d=1}^{r_{I}}I^{d}
such that
I^{d}
corresponds to the set of positive simple roots in the
d
-th Levi block of
L_{n,I}
. Hence,
I^{d}
is either empty or a maximal subinterval of
I
with
n_{d}=\#I^{d}+1
, and moreover
i<i^{\prime}
for each
i\in I^{d}
and
i^{\prime}\in I^{d^{\prime}}
satisfying
1\leq d<d^{\prime}\leq r_{I}
.

Let
I_{0}\subseteq I\subseteq I_{1}
be a subset satisfying
\#I=\ell
and
\underline{k}=\{k_{d}\}_{1\leq d\leq r_{I}}
be a tuple satisfying
|\underline{k}|=k
. Let
v\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{n,I}
with
\#v=k_{0}
and
\underline{\Lambda}\in\Sigma_{n,I,\underline{k}}
(see (
2.8
)), we use in the following the shortened notation
\Theta=(v,I,\underline{k},\underline{\Lambda})
and write
x_{\Theta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}v\otimes_{E}v_{n,I,\underline{k}}^{\underline{\Lambda}}
. So we always have
I_{0}\subseteq I\subseteq I_{v}\cap I_{1}
.

The space
E_{1,I_{0},I_{1}}^{-\ell,k}
admits a basis of the form

\{x_{\Theta}\mid\Theta=(v,I,\underline{k},\underline{\Lambda}),~v\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{n,\emptyset},~\underline{\Lambda}\in\Sigma_{n,I,\underline{k}},~I_{0}\subseteq I\subseteq I_{v}\cap I_{1},~|\underline{k}|=k,~\#I=\ell,~|v|=k_{0}\}.

Hence, we have a decomposition (with
E_{1,I_{0},I_{1},v}^{-\ell,k}
the
v
-isotypic direct summand)

E_{1,I_{0},I_{1}}^{-\ell,k}=\bigoplus_{v\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{n,\emptyset}}E_{1,I_{0},I_{1},v}^{-\ell,k}

which is compatible with the differential
d_{1,I_{0},I_{1}}^{-\ell,k}
.
For each
x\in E_{1,I_{0},I_{1},v}^{-\ell,k}
and each
\Theta=(v,I,\underline{k},\underline{\Lambda})
, we write
c_{\Theta}(x)
for the coefficient of
x
attached to
x_{\Theta}
.

We introduce two fundamental construction which (if defined) sends a given tuple
\Theta=(v,I,\underline{k},\underline{\Lambda})
to a new tuple.

•

For each
i\in\Delta_{n}\setminus I
, there exists a unique
1\leq d\leq r_{I}
such that
i=\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d}n_{d^{\prime}}
, and we define
p_{i}^{+}(\Theta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}(v,I\sqcup\{i\},\underline{k}^{\prime},\underline{\Lambda}^{\prime})
by the condition that

\Lambda^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}=\Lambda_{d^{\prime}}
for each
1\leq d^{\prime}\leq d-1
,
\Lambda^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}=\Lambda_{d^{\prime}+1}
for each
d+1\leq d^{\prime}\leq r_{I}-1
and
\Lambda^{\prime}_{d}=\Lambda_{d}\sqcup\Lambda_{d+1}
. So
p_{i}^{+}(\Theta)
is well defined if and only if
i\in\Delta_{n}\setminus I
and
\Lambda_{d}\cap\Lambda_{d+1}=\emptyset
.

•

For each
i\in I
, there exists a unique
1\leq d\leq r_{I}
such that
i\in I^{d}
, and we define
p_{i}^{-}(\Theta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}(v,I\setminus\{i\},\underline{k}^{\prime},\underline{\Lambda}^{\prime})
by the condition that

\Lambda^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}=\Lambda_{d^{\prime}}
for each
1\leq d^{\prime}\leq d-1
,
\Lambda^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}=\Lambda_{d^{\prime}-1}
for each
d+2\leq d^{\prime}\leq r_{I}+1
,
\Lambda^{\prime}_{d}=\Lambda_{d}
and
\Lambda^{\prime}_{d+1}=\emptyset
. So
p_{i}^{-}(\Theta)
is well defined if and only if
i\in I
and
\max\{m\mid(m,\iota)\in\Lambda_{d}\}\leq 2(i-\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d-1}n_{d^{\prime}})-1
.

Remark 2.11
.

For each
1\leq d\leq r_{I}
, we call
\Lambda_{d}
the
d
-th block of the tuple
\Theta
.
Intuitively speaking,
p_{i}^{+}(\Theta)
(when defined) is obtained from
\Theta
by gluing two adjacent blocks separated by
i
, and
p_{i}^{-}(\Theta)
(when defined) is obtained from
\Theta
by splitting a block into two adjacent blocks separated by
i
, leaving the second block trivial.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
\Theta=(v,I,\underline{k},\underline{\Lambda})
,
\Theta^{\prime}=(v^{\prime},I^{\prime},\underline{k}^{\prime},\underline{\Lambda}^{\prime})
be two tuple. We say that
\Theta^{\prime}
is an improvement of
\Theta
with level
d
for some
1\leq d\leq r_{I}
, if there exists
i\in I^{d}\setminus I_{0}
and
i^{\prime}=\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d}n_{d^{\prime}}\in(I_{v}\cap I_{1})\setminus I
such that
\Theta^{\prime}=p_{i^{\prime}}^{+}(p_{i}^{-}(\Theta))
(which forces
v=v^{\prime}
,
\underline{k}=\underline{k}^{\prime}
and
\underline{\Lambda}=\underline{\Lambda}^{\prime}
). We say that
\Theta
is smaller than
\Theta^{\prime}
with level
\geq d
for some
1\leq d\leq r_{I}
, written
\Theta<_{d}\Theta^{\prime}
, if there exists a sequence of tuples
\Theta=\Theta^{0},\dots,\Theta^{m}=\Theta^{\prime}
such that
\Theta^{m^{\prime}}
is an improvement of
\Theta^{m^{\prime}-1}
with level
\geq d
for each
1\leq m^{\prime}\leq m
. We say that
\Theta
and
\Theta^{\prime}
are
equivalent
(with respect to
(I_{0},I_{1})
) if there exists
\Theta^{\prime\prime}
such that
\Theta<_{1}\Theta^{\prime\prime}
and
\Theta^{\prime}<_{1}\Theta^{\prime\prime}
.

For each
1\leq d\leq r_{I}
satisfying
I^{d}\cap I_{0}\neq\emptyset
, there exists a unique maximal possible
1\leq d^{\prime}\leq r_{I_{0}}
such that
\emptyset\neq I_{0}^{d^{\prime}}\subseteq I^{d}
and
I^{d}\setminus I_{0}^{d^{\prime}}=I^{d,-}\sqcup I^{d,+}
with
I^{d,-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\{i\in I^{d}\mid i<i^{\prime}\text{ for each }i^{\prime}\in I_{0}^{d^{\prime}}\}
and
I^{d,+}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\{i\in I^{d}\mid i>i^{\prime}\text{ for each }i^{\prime}\in I_{0}^{d^{\prime}}\}
. So we always have
I^{d,+}\cap I_{0}=\emptyset
but
I^{d,-}\cap I_{0}
might be non-empty. If
I^{d,-}\neq\emptyset
, then we have
I^{d,-}\setminus I_{0}\neq\emptyset
and set
n_{d}^{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\min\{i\mid i\in I^{d,-}\setminus I_{0}\}-\sum_{d^{\prime\prime}=1}^{d-1}n_{d^{\prime\prime}}\leq\#I^{d,-}
.

Note that the sets
I\subseteq I_{v}\cap I_{1}
determines a sequence

0=r_{v,I_{1},I}^{0}<r_{v,I_{1},I}^{1}<\cdots<r_{v,I_{1},I}^{r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}}=r_{I}

characterized by

\Delta_{n}\setminus(I_{v}\cap I_{1})=\{\sum_{d=1}^{r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s}}n_{d}\mid 1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}-1\}
.
Given
1\leq d\leq r_{I}
, note that
\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d-1}n_{d^{\prime}}\in\{0\}\sqcup\Delta_{n}\setminus(I_{v}\cap I_{1})
if and only if there exists
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
such that
d=r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s-1}+1
, and similarly
\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d}n_{d^{\prime}}\in\{n\}\sqcup\Delta_{n}\setminus(I_{v}\cap I_{1})
if and only if there exists
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
such that
d=r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s}
.

Definition 2.13
.

For each tuple
\Theta=(v,I,\underline{k},\underline{\Lambda})
, we define its
sign
as
\varepsilon(\Theta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}(-1)^{\sum_{i\in I}i}
.
We say that
\Theta
is
(I_{0},I_{1})
-standard
if there exists
2\leq d_{I_{0},I_{1},\Theta}\leq r_{I}
such that

•

\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d_{I_{0},I_{1},\Theta}-1}n_{d^{\prime}}\in(I_{v}\cap I_{1})\setminus I
;

•

\Lambda^{d_{I_{0},I_{1},\Theta}}=\emptyset
and
\Lambda^{d}\neq\emptyset
for each
d_{I_{0},I_{1},\Theta}+1\leq d\leq r_{I}
;

•

I^{d_{I_{0},I_{1},\Theta}}\subseteq I_{0}
; and

•

there does not exist
\Theta^{\prime}
such that
\Theta<_{d_{I_{0},I_{1},\Theta}}\Theta^{\prime}
.

We say that
\Theta
is
maximally
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic
if

•

there does not exist
\Theta^{\prime}
such that
\Theta<_{1}\Theta^{\prime}
;

•

if
k_{d}=0
(namely
\Lambda_{d}=\emptyset
) for some
1\leq d\leq r_{I}
, then there exists
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
such that
d=r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s-1}+1
;

•

for each
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
satisfying
I^{r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s}}\cap I_{0}\neq\emptyset
,
\Lambda_{r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s}}\neq\emptyset
and
(\{2n_{r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s}}-1\}\times S)\cap\Lambda_{r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s}}=\emptyset
, we have
I^{r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s},+}=\emptyset
and there exists
n^{\prime}\geq\#I^{r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s},-}+1
and
\iota\in S
such that
(2n^{\prime}-1,\iota)\in\Lambda_{r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s}}
; and

•

for each
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
satisfying
I^{r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s}}\cap I_{0}=\emptyset
and
\Lambda_{r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s}}\neq\emptyset
, there exists
\iota\in S
such that
(2n_{r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s}}-1,\iota)\in\Lambda_{r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s}}
.

We say that
\Theta
is
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic
if there exists a maximally
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic
\Theta^{\prime}
such that
\Theta<_{1}\Theta^{\prime}
.
For each equivalent class
\Omega
of
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic tuples, we associate an
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atom

x_{\Omega}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}\sum_{\Theta=(v,I,\underline{k},\underline{\Lambda})\in\Omega}\varepsilon(\Theta)x_{\Theta}.

The
bidegree
of a
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atom
x_{\Omega}
is defined to be
(-\ell_{\Omega},k_{\Omega})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}(-\#I,|\underline{k}|)
for an arbitrary
\Theta=(v,I,\underline{k},\underline{\Lambda})\in\Omega
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
\Theta=(v,I,\underline{k},\underline{\Lambda})
be a tuple as before and
1\leq d\leq r_{I}
be an integer. If
I^{d}\cap I_{0}=\emptyset
, then the following statements are equivalent

(i)

there exists
i\in I^{d}
such that
\mathrm{Res}_{n,I,I\setminus\{i\}}^{k}(x_{\Theta})\neq 0
;

(ii)

n_{d}\geq 2
and
\mathrm{Res}_{n,I,I\setminus\{i\}}^{k}(x_{\Theta})\neq 0
for
i\in\{-1+\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d}n_{d^{\prime}},1+\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d-1}n_{d^{\prime}}\}
;

(iii)

n_{d}\geq 2
and
(\{2n_{d}-1\}\times S)\cap\Lambda_{d}=\emptyset
.

If
I^{d}\cap I_{0}\neq\emptyset
, then the following statements are equivalent

(iv)

there exists
i\in I^{d,+}
such that
\mathrm{Res}_{n,I,I\setminus\{i\}}^{k}(x_{\Theta})\neq 0
;

(v)

I^{d,+}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathrm{Res}_{n,I,I\setminus\{i\}}^{k}(x_{\Theta})\neq 0
for
i=-1+\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d}n_{d^{\prime}}
;

(vi)

I^{d,+}\neq\emptyset
and
(\{2n_{d}-1\}\times S)\cap\Lambda_{d}=\emptyset
.

If
I^{d}\cap I_{0}\neq\emptyset
and
I^{d,+}=\emptyset
, then the following statements are equivalent

(vii)

there exists
i\in I^{d,-}
such that
c_{p_{i}^{-}(\Theta)}(\mathrm{Res}_{n,I,I\setminus\{i\}}^{k}(x_{\Theta}))\neq 0
;

(viii)

I^{d,-}\neq\emptyset
and
c_{p_{i}^{-}(\Theta)}(\mathrm{Res}_{n,I,I\setminus\{i\}}^{k}(x_{\Theta}))\neq 0
for
i=\#I^{d,-}+\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d-1}n_{d^{\prime}}
);

(ix)

I^{d,-}\neq\emptyset
and
\max\{m\mid(m,\iota)\in\Lambda_{d}\}<2\#I^{d,-}+1
.

Proof.

We only treat the case
I^{d}\cap I_{0}=\emptyset
and the other two cases are similar. For each
i\in I^{d}
, it follows from Lemma
2.8
that
\mathrm{Res}_{n,I,I\setminus\{i\}}^{k}(x_{\Theta})\neq 0
if and only if
\mathbf{D}_{n_{d},\Lambda_{d},\overline{i}}\neq\emptyset
where
\overline{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{def}}}}{{=}}i-\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d-1}n_{d^{\prime}}
. Note that
\mathbf{D}_{n_{d},\Lambda_{d},\overline{i}}\neq\emptyset
implies the existence of
\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda
such that
\max\{m\mid(m,\iota)\in\Lambda^{\prime}\}\leq 2\overline{i}-1
and
\max\{m\mid(m,\iota)\in\Lambda_{d}\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}\}\leq 2(n_{d}-\overline{i})-1
, which altogether implies that
\max\{m\mid(m,\iota)\in\Lambda_{d}\}\leq 2n_{d}-3
(which is equivalent to
(\{2n_{d}-1\}\times S)\cap\Lambda_{d}=\emptyset
). In other words, we have
(i)
implies
(iii)
. As
(iii)
clearly implies
(ii)
(namely
\mathbf{D}_{n_{d},\Lambda_{d},n_{d}-1}\neq\emptyset\neq\mathbf{D}_{n_{d},\Lambda_{d},1}
) and
(ii)
clearly implies
(i)
, we finish the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
\Theta=(v,I,\underline{k},\underline{\Lambda})
be a maximally
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic tuple. Let
1\leq d\leq r_{I}
be an integer. Then we have

•

If
I^{d}\cap I_{0}=\emptyset
and
(\{2n_{d}-1\}\times S)\cap\Lambda_{d}=\emptyset
, then
n_{d}=1
,
\Lambda_{d}=\emptyset
and there exists
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
such that
d=r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s-1}+1
.

•

If
I^{d}\cap I_{0}\neq\emptyset
and
I^{d,+}\neq\emptyset
, then there exists
\iota\in S
such that
(2n_{d}-1,\iota)\in\Lambda_{d}
;

•

If
I^{d}\cap I_{0}\neq\emptyset
,
I^{d,+}=\emptyset
and
\max\{m\mid(m,\iota)\in\Lambda_{d}\}<2\#I^{d,-}+1
, then
I^{d,-}=\emptyset
,
\Lambda_{d}=\emptyset
and there exists
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
such that
d=r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s-1}+1
.

Proof.

We may assume throughout that
d\neq r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s}
for each
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
(see Definition
2.13
).
We treat the three claims separately

•

Let
d
satisfy
I^{d}\cap I_{0}=\emptyset
and
(\{2n_{d}-1\}\times S)\cap\Lambda_{d}=\emptyset
. If
n_{d}\geq 2
, then
\Theta<p_{i_{2}}^{+}p_{i_{1}}^{-}(\Theta)
for
i_{1}=-1+\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d}n_{d^{\prime}}
and
i_{2}=\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d}n_{d^{\prime}}
(using Lemma
2.14
), which contradicts the maximality of
\Theta
. Hence, we have
n_{d}=1
,
k_{d}=0
and
\Lambda_{d}=\emptyset
, which gives a
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
such that
d=r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s-1}+1
by Definition
2.13
.

•

Let
d
satisfy
I^{d}\cap I_{0}\neq\emptyset
and
I^{d,+}\neq\emptyset
. If
(\{2n_{d}-1\}\times S)\cap\Lambda_{d}=\emptyset
, then Lemma
2.14
implies that
\Theta<p_{i_{4}}^{+}p_{i_{3}}^{-}(\Theta)
for
i_{3}=-1+\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d}n_{d^{\prime}}
and
i_{4}=\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d}n_{d^{\prime}}
, which contradicts the maximality of
\Theta
.

•

Let
d
satisfy
I^{d}\cap I_{0}\neq\emptyset
,
I^{d,+}=\emptyset
and
\max\{m\mid(m,\iota)\in\Lambda_{d}\}<2\#I^{d,-}+1
. If
I^{d,-}\neq\emptyset
, then Lemma
2.14
implies that
\Theta<p_{i_{6}}^{+}p_{i_{5}}^{-}(\Theta)
for

i_{5}=-1-\#I^{d}\cap I_{0}+\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d}n_{d^{\prime}}=\#I^{d,-}+\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d-1}n_{d^{\prime}}

and
i_{6}=\sum_{d^{\prime}=1}^{d}n_{d^{\prime}}
, which contradicts the maximality of
\Theta
. Hence, we have
I^{d,-}=\emptyset
, which together with
\max\{m\mid(m,\iota)\in\Lambda_{d}\}<2\#I^{d,-}+1
forces
k_{d}=0
(namely
\Lambda_{d}=\emptyset
), and thus there exists
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
such that
d=r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s-1}+1
by Definition
2.13
.

∎

Now we classify
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic tuples when
k\in\{\ell+\#I_{1}-2\#I_{0},\ell+\#I_{1}-2\#I_{0}+1\}
.

Lemma 2.16
.

We have the following consequences of existence of
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic tuples.

•

If
\Theta=(v,I,\underline{k},\underline{\Lambda})
is
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic, then we have

\ell-2\#I_{0}+\#I_{1}\leq k\leq\ell-n+1+[K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}](n^{2}-1).

•

If
k=\ell+\#I_{1}-2\#I_{0}
, then
I_{v}\cup I_{1}=\Delta_{n}
and a maximally
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic
\Theta
satisfies

–

there does not exist
i\in\Delta_{n}\setminus I_{v}
such that
\#v\cap\mathcal{B}_{n,\Delta_{n}\setminus\{i\}}\geq 2
(which implies that
k_{0}=\#\Delta_{n}\setminus I_{v}
);

–

if
I^{d}\cap I_{0}=\emptyset
, then
\Lambda_{d}\neq\{(2n_{d}-1,\iota)\}
for any
\iota\in S
if and only if
n_{d}=1
,
\Lambda_{d}=\emptyset
and there exists
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
such that
d=r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s-1}+1
;

–

if
I^{d}\cap I_{0}\neq\emptyset
, then
I^{d}\cap I_{0}
is an interval and
I^{d,+}=\emptyset
, and moreover
\Lambda_{d}\neq\{(2\#I^{d,+}+1,\iota)\}
for any
\iota\in S
if and only if
I^{d,-}=\emptyset
,
\Lambda_{d}=\emptyset
and there exists
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
such that
d=r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s-1}+1
.

•

If
k=\ell+\#I_{1}-2\#I_{0}+1
, then either
\#I_{v}\cup I_{1}=n-2
and a maximally
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic
\Theta
satisfies exactly the same three conditions as
k=\ell+\#I_{1}-2\#I_{0}
case, or
I_{v}\cup I_{1}=\Delta_{n}
and a maximally
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic
\Theta
satisfies the conditions in
k=\ell+\#I_{1}-2\#I_{0}
case with exactly one of the following modifications

–

there exists exactly one
i\in\Delta_{n}\setminus I_{v}
such that
\#v\cap\mathcal{B}_{n,\Delta_{n}\setminus\{i\}}\geq 2
and the equality holds for that
i
;

–

for exactly one choice of
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
and
d=r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s-1}+1
, we have
I^{d,+}=\emptyset\neq I^{d,-}
and there exists
\iota\in S
such that
\Lambda_{d}=\{(2\#I^{d,-}+1,\iota)\}
if
I^{d}\cap I_{0}\neq\emptyset
and
\Lambda_{d}=\{(2n_{d}-1,\iota)\}
if
I^{d}\cap I_{0}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

It is harmless to assume that our
\Theta
is maximally
(I_{0},I_{1})
-atomic (see Definition
2.12
and Definition
2.13
).
We start with the trivial observation that
r_{I_{v}}-1\leq k_{0}\leq([K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]+1)(r_{I_{v}}-1)\leq([K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]+1)(r_{I}-1)
, which implies that

\sum_{d=0}^{r_{I}}k_{d}\leq k_{0}+\sum_{d=1}^{r_{I}}[K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}](n_{d}^{2}-1)=k_{0}+[K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}](-r_{I}+\sum_{d=1}^{r_{I}}n_{d}^{2})\\
\leq([K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]+1)(r_{I}-1)+[K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}](n^{2}-3r_{I}+2)=(1-2[K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}])(r_{I}-1)+[K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}](n^{2}-1)\\
\leq\ell-n+1+[K:\mathbb{Q}_{p}](n^{2}-1).

We have the following observations from Lemma
2.15
.

•

If
I^{d}\cap I_{0}=\emptyset
and there does not exist
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
such that
d=r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s-1}+1
, then
k_{d}\geq 2n_{d}-1
and the equality holds if and only if
\Lambda_{d}=\{(2n_{d}-1,\iota)\}
for some
\iota\in S
.

•

If
I^{d}\cap I_{0}=\emptyset
and there exists
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
such that
d=r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s-1}+1
, then either
n_{d}=1
(with
k_{d}=0
) or
k_{d}\geq 2n_{d}-1
. So we always have
k_{d}\geq 2n_{d}-2
and the equality holds if and only if
n_{d}=1
.

•

If
I^{d}\cap I_{0}\neq\emptyset
and there does not exist
1\leq s\leq r_{I_{v}\cap I_{1}}
such that
d=r_{v,I_{1},I}^{s-1}+1
, then
k_{d}\geq 2\#I^{d,-}+1\geq 2n_{d}-2\#I^{d}\cap I_{0}-1
